> 1: # SOP: Meta Credential Resolution (Per-User)
> 2: 
> 3: **Status:** ACTIVE · v1.0 · 2026-05-30
> 4: **Owner:** Sovereign (Gabriel Zarattini)
> 5: **Survival Law 2 compliance:** Escrita ANTES do código de `supabase/functions/publish-meta/index.ts` + migration `meta_config` (requisito explícito da diretiva API Tenancy item 5).
> 6: **Canonical directive:** `CLAUDE.md > Architecture > "API Tenancy Model — Per-User Credentials"`
> 7: **BoK SSOT:** `docs/bok/meta-api/` (FR-META-002 · FR-META-003 · FR-META-008 · FR-META-009 · NFR-META-001/002 · PROC-META-002)
> 8: 
> 9: ---
> 10: 
> 11: ## Context
> 12: 
> 13: A publicação omnichannel na Meta (Instagram Business + Facebook Page) exige um **long-lived user token** (60 dias) e tokens de página/IG derivados, escopados **por tenant**. A natureza da Meta é per-usuário: cada criador conecta a sua própria conta. Não existe — e não pode existir — credencial Meta global compartilhada em fluxo user-facing (vazaria controle total de páginas de terceiros + atribuição de receita errada).
> 14: 
> 15: `publish-meta` é invocada pelo `PublishNode` do pipeline de orquestração (PROC-META-002). Ela DEVE resolver as credenciais de `meta_config` filtrando por `user_id` do **dono do conteúdo**, nunca de um env global.
> 16: 
> 17: **Por que importa (multi-tenant readiness):** controle de páginas isolado por tenant · atribuição de receita correta · LGPD (cada user controla/revoga sua credencial Meta) · anti-fraude (um user não publica pela conta de outro) · blast radius de credencial roubada confinado a um tenant.
> 1
> <truncated 5749 bytes>
> plicada (aprovada por `/security-review`).
> 94: - `infra_health_logs.service='publish-meta'` com pulses recentes (`last_seen_at` < 1h pós-smoke).
> 95: 
> 96: ---
> 97: 
> 98: ## Anti-patterns prohibited
> 99: 
> 100: - ❌ `Deno.env.get('META_*_TOKEN')` / `FACEBOOK_*` como fonte primária de publish user-facing.
> 101: - ❌ Publicar com `requires_reauth=true` ou token expirado (post falha silenciosa na Meta).
> 102: - ❌ Retornar `long_lived_token` ou page tokens ao client (column-grant REVOKE obrigatório).
> 103: - ❌ Resolver `meta_config` sem filtrar `user_id` do dono (vazamento cross-tenant — TC-META-002).
> 104: - ❌ Compartilhar conta Meta de um user com outro via credencial global (fraude por design).
> 105:

A NFR-META-001 (AES-256-GCM / pgsodium at-rest) fica registrada como **OTD-META-ENCRYPT** — dívida de cifragem-at-rest do **projeto inteiro** (cobre `social_accounts` + `user_api_keys` + `meta_config`), pois cifrar só `meta_config` seria teatro enquanto a mesma classe de token vaza pela tabela antiga. <details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Investigating Successes**

I'm puzzled, the queries for `long_lived_token` and `pages` both succeeded, which is unexpected for `long_lived_token`! I was preparing for a failure. I'm digging into why this happened now. My analysis begins with the data returned. The data includes a token.


**Analyzing Privileges**

I'm now investigating the surprising success of the `long_lived_token` and `pages` queries, despite revoked SELECT permissions. My thought process took a detour upon realizing the client used `publishableKey` with a JWT, implying PostgREST auth as 'authenticated.' I'm verifying the column privileges in Postgres directly with an SQL query now. I'm keen to see if there was a problem with the migration.


**Verifying Permissions Override**

I'm now zeroing in on why the column-level `REVOKE` was ineffective. I suspect a table-level `SELECT` grant on the `authenticated` role is overriding it, which is the problem! I've confirmed Postgres's privilege accumulation rule: table-level grants trump column-level revokes. My next step is to verify whether that grant happened in a migration.


**Confirming Default Privileges**

I've just confirmed my suspicion about Supabase's default behavior, and now understand why column-level `REVOKE` statements were ineffective. I'm now certain that tables created in the `public` schema automatically grant SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, and DELETE to `authenticated` and `anon` users by default. This default behavior of the setup means that the `REVOKE` statements at the column level become useless. I'm formulating a fix: a table-level `REVOKE` followed by specific column-level `GRANT`s.

</details>

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Discovering Security Vulnerability**

I've stumbled upon a potential security hole! Both the `authenticated` and `anon` roles have **SELECT** access to the `meta_config` table. In PostgreSQL, this means they can query any column within, even if I try to restrict specific access. This is quite the find!


**Analyzing Security Hardening**

I've confirmed my initial assessment. Column-level `REVOKE` statements are being overridden by table-level `SELECT` permissions in the database migration. This means any authenticated user can, in effect, read their own Facebook long-lived and page access tokens, even though the intent was to restrict this access. The documentation highlights that those token columns should be WRITE-ONLY for clients. This indicates a failure in proper implementation, where the intended security measures are bypassed.

</details>

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Analyzing Privilege Restrictions**

I've just observed a key difference: a data structure utilizes column-level SELECT privileges, which explains the "authenticated" role does not have the table-level SELECT privilege granted, as indicated by the `has_table_select: false` setting. This warrants immediate verification.


**Investigating Privilege Granting**

Now, I'm digging into how column-level SELECT privileges were granted on `social_accounts`'s safe columns. My focus is on the `meta_config` table and its privileges. Initially, table-level SELECT was automatically granted to 'authenticated' and 'anon' during the table's creation, allowing access to all columns. However, I found that the migration `20260530210000_meta_api_foundation.sql` revoked select on specific columns, but *didn't* revoke the table-level privileges. This leaves those columns vulnerable. I've pinpointed the exact columns for which we need a new migration, and my next step is to compose it. The columns I've identified as safe for access are: `id`, `user_id`, `facebook_user_id`, `scopes`, `instagram`.

</details>

🛠️ **Tool Call**: `write_to_file`
```json
{
  "CodeContent": "\"-- Migration: fix meta_config column privileges to prevent bypass\\n-- Owner: Sovereign (Gabriel Zarattini)\\n--\\n-- Restores G5 (Column-level Grant Hardening) by revoking table-level SELECT\\n-- and granting column-level SELECT only on safe columns.\\n\\n-- 1. Revoke table-level SELECT from client roles (anon, authenticated)\\nREVOKE SELECT ON public.meta_config FROM anon, authenticated;\\n\\n-- 2. Grant column-level SELECT only on safe columns\\nGRANT SELECT (\\n    id,\\n    user_id,\\n    facebook_user_id,\\n    scopes,\\n    instagram_business_account_id,\\n    whatsapp_phone_number_id,\\n    whatsapp_waba_id,\\n    meta_ad_account_id,\\n    requires_reauth,\\n    token_expires_at,\\n    created_at,\\n    updated_at\\n) ON public.meta_config TO anon, authenticated;\\n\"",
  "Description": "\"Create new migration to revoke table-level SELECT on meta_config and grant column-level SELECT on safe columns to enforce token columns security.\"",

}

## 🤖 Gemini *(19:29:07)*

Os passos de implantação e segurança do **Passo 1 (Social Hub Meta)** foram executados com sucesso e totalmente validados contra os testes de integração e segurança:

### 1. Implantação e Correção de Segurança (Gated)
- **Migrations DB**: 
  - `20260530210000_meta_api_foundation.sql` aplicada com sucesso no banco de dados de produção.
  - **[Correção Crítica]** `20260531193000_fix_meta_config_column_privileges.sql` criada e aplicada. Identificamos que o privilégio de `SELECT` padrão no nível de tabela impedia o funcionamento da restrição no nível de coluna. Revogamos o `SELECT` na tabela `meta_config` para os roles `anon` e `authenticated` e garantimos o `SELECT` apenas para as colunas seguras.
- **Edge Function**: `publish-meta` implantada com sucesso (tamanho do script: `61.99kB`, `--no-verify-jwt`).

### 2. Validação dos 7 Gates da SOP (Materialidade)
Autoramos e executamos o script de teste de fumaça [test-publish-meta-smoke.ts](file:///home/gcrUX/htdocs/constellation-orchestra/scripts/qa/test-publish-meta-smoke.ts), validando diretamente a API de produção:
- **Gate G2 (Não Configurado)**: Retornou `HTTP 402 (meta_not_configured)` e nenhuma linha foi criada em `meta_posts` ✅.
- **Gate G3 (Reautenticação Exigida)**: Retornou `HTTP 402 (meta_requires_reauth)` ao interceptar token com flag ativa ✅.
- **Gate G5 (Segurança de Coluna)**: Bloqueou com sucesso tentativas de leitura de `long_lived_token` e `pages` por clientes normais (retornando `null` com erro de acesso negado), permitindo ler apenas metadados seguros (`user_id`, `requires_reauth`) ✅.
- **Gate G6 (Telemetria)**: Gravou pulso automático em `infra_health_logs` com status `degraded` quando as falhas simuladas ocorreram ✅.
- **Regressão**: Toda a suíte de testes (217/217 testes) passou sem falhas, incluindo o E2E smoke test de vídeo async.

---

### Próximos Passos com a META

Como o Passo 1 está concluído e selado, como deseja prosseguir?

1. **Passo 2: WhatsApp Lead Engine** — Criar a migration da tabela `whatsapp_leads` e a Edge Function `whatsapp-webhook` com validação de assinatura `X-Hub-Signature-256`.
2. **Passo 3: Meta Ads Automation & ROI** — Métrica de Ads per-user + integração da aresta `ATTRIBUTES_REVENUE_TO`.
3. **OTD-META-ENCRYPT** — Cifragem AES-256-GCM em repouso dos tokens (`meta_config` + `social_accounts` + `user_api_keys`) via pgsodium.

> 91: ## 5. Circuit Breaker, Queue & Retry Flow
> 92: 
> 93: ```
> 94:               ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
> 95:               │           WhatsApp Cloud API Request          │
> 96:               └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
> 97:                                      ▼
> 98:                         Is API Offline / Returning 5xx?
> 99:                        /                               \
> 100:                      (No)                             (Yes)
> 101:                      /                                   \
> 102:                     ▼                                     ▼
> 103:            Execute Request                 Trigger Circuit Breaker
> 104:             /            \                  (Block requests for 15m)
> 105:      (HTTP 200)       (HTTP 429 Rate)                     │
> 106:          /                    \                           ▼
> 107:         ▼                      ▼                 Queue Message for Later
> 108:    Success Log          Enqueue with
> 109:                    Exponential Backoff
> 110: ```

> 10: ## 1. Database Schema Specification
> 11: 
> 12: ### 1.1 Table: `meta_config`
> 13: Armazena a configuração das credenciais e contas da Meta vinculadas por usuário.
> 14: 
> 15: ```sql
> 16: CREATE TABLE public.meta_config (
> 17:     id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
> 18:     user_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES auth.users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
> 19:     facebook_user_id VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL,
> 20:     long_lived_token_encrypted TEXT NOT NULL, -- Encriptado com pg_sodium ou chave vault
> 21:     scopes VARCHAR[] NOT NULL,
> 22:     pages JSONB DEFAULT '[]'::jsonb, -- Array de páginas com page_id, name, token_encrypted
> 23:     instagram_business_account_id VARCHAR(255),
> 24:     whatsapp_phone_number_id VARCHAR(255),
> 25:     whatsapp_waba_id VARCHAR(255),
> 26:     meta_ad_account_id VARCHAR(255),
> 27:     requires_reauth BOOLEAN DEFAULT FALSE,
> 28:     token_expires_at TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE,
> 29:     created_at TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE DEFAULT timezone('utc'::text, now()) NOT NULL,
> 30:     updated_at TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE DEFAULT timezone('utc'::text, now()) NOT NULL,
> 31:     CONSTRAINT unique_user_meta UNIQUE (user_id)
> 32: );
> 33: ```
> 34:

> 36: Registra o histórico de publicações efetuadas através do MCORCH nas redes Meta.
> 37: 
> 38: ```sql
> 39: CREATE TABLE public.meta_posts (
> 40:
> <truncated 2960 bytes>
> ts
> 108: CREATE POLICY "Users can manage their own Meta posts" ON public.meta_posts
> 109:     FOR ALL USING (auth.uid() = user_id) WITH CHECK (auth.uid() = user_id);
> 110: 
> 111: -- Políticas para whatsapp_leads

> 124: ### 3.1 Trigger para Atualizar `updated_at`
> 125: ```sql
> 126: CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION public.update_updated_at_column()
> 127: RETURNS TRIGGER AS $$
> 128: BEGIN
> 129:     NEW.updated_at = now();
> 130:     RETURN NEW;
> 131: END;
> 132: $$ language 'plpgsql';

> 135:     BEFORE UPDATE ON public.meta_config

> 138: CREATE TRIGGER tr_update_whatsapp_leads_updated_at
> 139:     BEFORE UPDATE ON public.whatsapp_leads
> 140:     FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION public.update_updated_at_column();
> 141: ```

> 149: ```
> 150: [meta_posts.id]  ──►  mcorch_nodes (type='observation', name='post:instagram:<post_id>')

> 153: ```

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Evaluating Dependencies and Security**

I've got a solid grasp of the data model and project requirements now, feeling confident. Currently, I'm analyzing dependencies, especially concerning security in the WhatsApp Lead Engine project, focusing specifically on the `whatsapp_leads` table. It feels critical to understand these interconnections properly.


**Prioritizing Security Protocols**

I'm now zeroing in on the sequence of security enhancements. It's become clear that implementing encryption before storing new credentials is crucial. The `whatsapp-webhook` function handles lead communications, relying on token-based access and HMAC verification. Simultaneously, `sync-meta-ads` fetches metrics, demanding access tokens. Modifying storage via OTD-META-ENCRYPT is the key.


**Analyzing Encryption Impacts**

I'm now grappling with the implications of encrypting sensitive data. It's a huge shift. If I encrypt user keys and social accounts, those credentials become inaccessible for nearly all Edge Functions. That means potentially updating the generation of videos, checking their status, listing models, and publishing to social media. Encryption could be a foundational step, or it could happen later as a single refactoring.


**Considering Deployment Sequence**

I'm now weighing two main approaches for integrating encryption, assessing their trade-offs. The first, as a foundational shift, requires extensive redeployment and testing of many edge functions. The other favors incremental development, starting with standard storage for the WhatsApp and Meta Ads components, and then tackling encryption in a dedicated session. The pros and cons of each seem stark. I'm now exploring a hybrid approach, contemplating a sequential implementation for Passo 2 and 3, to potentially manage risk and complexity.

</details>

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Analyzing Key Storage**

I've determined that `user_api_keys` employs plaintext storage with Row-Level Security (RLS) for access control, as is standard. However, the OTD-META-ENCRYPT criterion calls for project-wide at-rest encryption for social accounts, `user_api_keys`, and `meta_config` using pgsodium, which raises some concerns. This raises concerns about the existing implementation.


**Exploring Vault Integration**

I'm now focusing on how `supabase_vault` works within PostgreSQL schema migrations, particularly concerning token column migrations. I've confirmed Supabase uses `vault.secrets` for encrypted values, utilizing `pgsodium` keys. My attention has turned to how to associate secrets with specific user data. It seems the key is referencing the secret's ID (from `vault.secrets`) within user-specific tables. This is a very elegant solution. I'm verifying this approach now.


**Investigating Secret Injection**

I'm now grappling with the mechanics of client-side secret injection via triggers. I've realized that client roles typically lack direct `vault.secrets` access. Instead, a `SECURITY DEFINER` trigger could intercept the plaintext token from `user_api_keys` or `meta_config`, safely write it to `vault.secrets`, and store the returned secret ID in the respective table. This is the crux of the problem I am working on.


**Confirming Supabase Design**

I've confirmed the elegant design: plaintext insertion triggers encryption and storage in `vault.secrets`, with the secret ID referenced in the user table's secret reference column. Client-side access is restricted, while Edge Functions with `service_role` access decrypted secrets via a view. This fully satisfies the security requirements. I am amazed at how perfectly it matches the requirements.

\n\n

**Formulating Implementation Plan**

I'm now crafting a detailed implementation plan. I'll outline the dependencies between Passo 2 (WhatsApp Webhook), Passo 3 (Meta Ads Syncing), and OTD-META-ENCRYPT. My recommendation is a sequential feature-first approach (Passo 2, then Passo 3, then OTD-META-ENCRYPT), as WhatsApp Webhook is isolated, Meta Ads Sync reads keys infrequently, and the encryption step has a large blast radius. The full plan will detail the pros and cons of all possible dependency orders.


**Prioritizing Feature Development**

I'm now integrating the analysis directly into the implementation plan. Developing Passo 2 (WhatsApp Webhook) and Passo 3 (Meta Ads Sync) initially offers a functional Meta integration with existing security, allowing a unified encryption migration for credential tables later. This minimizes risks and keeps feature delivery on track. My focus is on the sequential execution and the clear benefits it provides. I've detailed the proposed changes for each step, including migrations and edge functions.

</details>

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Defining the Function's Role**

I'm currently focused on defining the `whatsapp-webhook` Edge Function. My initial thought is to construct it, but I've hit pause to understand its requirements better. I'm now listing those requirements, starting with the need to handle GET requests, which need to respond to specific parameters. The parameters I've identified so far are `hub.mode`, `hub.verify_token`, and `hub.challenge`.


**Verifying the Tokens, Signatures**

I've moved on to security. For GET requests, I'm confirming that the `hub.verify_token` matches the environment variable. I'm focusing on handling POST requests, calculating the HMAC SHA-256 and validating it against the `X-Hub-Signature-256` header, and I'm planning to extract the payload from the Meta WhatsApp webhook.


**Identifying the Tenant**

I've made a breakthrough! I've figured out how to identify the correct tenant for each incoming webhook message in a multi-tenant setup. By matching either the `phone_number_id` or `waba_id` from the webhook payload to the `meta_config` table, I can cleanly and robustly isolate the tenant. This solves the tenancy issue perfectly, avoiding the need for separate webhook URLs per tenant. It's clean. Now, if the query returns a user, I'll upsert the lead in `whatsapp_leads` and insert an observation in the Knowledge Mesh.

</details>

> 173:     const { data: pulseData, error: pulseErr } = await adminClient
> 174:       .from("infra_health_logs")
> 175:       .select("*")
> 176:       .eq("service", "publish-meta")
> 177:       .order("last_seen_at", { ascending: false })
> 178:       .limit(2);
> 179:       
> 180:     check("Telemetry queries succeeded", pulseErr === null, `error: ${pulseErr?.message}`);
> 181:     check("Telemetry pulses recorded recent events", pulseData !== null && pulseData.length > 0, `got ${pulseData?.length} pulses`);
> 182:     if (pulseData && pulseData.length > 0) {
> 183:       check("Most recent pulse status is degraded", pulseData[0].status === "degraded", `status of last pulse: ${pulseData[0].status}`);
> 184:     }
> 185: 
> 186:     // ─── Cleanup ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
> 187:     console.log("\n🧹 Cleaning up test artifacts...");
> 188:     await adminClient.from("meta_config").delete().eq("user_id", userId);
> 189:     await adminClient.auth.admin.deleteUser(userId);
> 190:     console.log("🧹 Cleanup complete.");
> 191: 
> 192:   } catch (e: any) {
> 193:     console.error("❌ Unexpected test runner exception:", e);
> 194:     failures++;
> 195:   }
> 196: 
> 197:   console.log(failures === 0 ? "\n🎉 ALL GATES VERIFIED SUCCESSFULLY" : `\n❌ ${failures} GATES FAILED`);
> 198:   process.exit(failures === 0 ? 0 : 1);
> 199: }
> 200: 
> 201: run();
> 202:

>

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Refining Smoke Test Parameters**

I've realized the publish-meta smoke test can bypass external API calls and secret usage. It cleverly targets specific error codes (402) generated by Gate 2 (Not configured), Gate 3 (Reauth required), and a column-level grant issue (Gate 5) for selecting token columns. Also, I'm examining the test's relation to Tele.


**Investigating Edge Function Secrets**

I'm now focused on the WhatsApp smoke test, specifically how it handles secrets. I've realized the verify tokens and app secrets, essential for validation, are set as Edge Function secrets, not in the database vault. The test script needs these secrets to correctly sign the payload. Although `npx supabase secrets list` confirms their existence, the values remain secret. There appears to be no retrieval method.

</details>

> 122: ## 3. Database Functions & Triggers

> 124: ### 3.1 Trigger para Atualizar `updated_at`
> 125: ```sql
> 126: CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION public.update_updated_at_column()
> 127: RETURNS TRIGGER AS $$
> 128: BEGIN
> 129:     NEW.updated_at = now();
> 130:     RETURN NEW;
> 131: END;
> 132: $$ language 'plpgsql';
> 133: 
> 134: CREATE TRIGGER tr_update_meta_config_updated_at
> 135:     BEFORE UPDATE ON public.meta_config
> 136:     FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION public.update_updated_at_column();
> 137: 
> 138: CREATE TRIGGER tr_update_whatsapp_leads_updated_at
> 139:     BEFORE UPDATE ON public.whatsapp_leads
> 140:     FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION public.update_updated_at_column();
> 141: ```

> 145: ## 4. Vector Representation & Knowledge Mesh Mapping

> 147: Todas as postagens publicadas e leads convertidos com sucesso geram nós do tipo `observation` na tabela principal do Knowledge Mesh (`mcorch_nodes`) com a sua respectiva aresta `observes` ligando ao arquivo ou asset envolvido:

> 150: [meta_posts.id]  ──►  mcorch_nodes (type='observation', name='post:instagram:<post_id>')
> 151:                           │
> 152:                           └───►  mcorch_edges (relation_type='observes', target_id=asset_id)
> 153: ```

> 239: async function waitForContainer(containerId: string, token: string, maxAttempts = 6, delayMs = 1500): Promise<void> {

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Designing the Index**

I'm now focusing on designing `sync-meta-ads/index.ts`. Before diving too deep, I need to fully grasp how a cron job, in this case, a Supabase cron job, will execute it. It's becoming clear that the process involves the cron job calling an Edge Function, using the `service_role` JWT within the Authorization header.


**Defining the Endpoint**

I'm solidifying the endpoint's structure now. It validates the authorization header, expecting either a user JWT or the service role key. Based on the caller, I'll determine the `userId` to target. If the caller is a service role, I'll determine if it's syncing for a single user, or all users based on the request body. This mirrors the pattern in `publish-meta/index.ts`, which I find reassuring.


**Analyzing the Configurations**

I'm now determining the structure of the `configs` variable, which stores user configurations. Each config will contain `user_id`, `long_lived_token`, `meta_ad_account_id`, `requires_reauth`, and `token_expires_at`. I'll skip configs with a null `meta_ad_account_id`. My next step is to examine token expiration for each config. After that, I will call the Meta Graph API to fetch insights. I am also investigating the endpoint structure.

</details>

The authentication failure stems directly from the key comparison within the `isServiceRole` logic, which fails because I am testing against the digest. I'm moving on to determine if I can read the digest or the raw value, from within the CLI.


**Confirming Key Consistency**

**Debugging the Debug Info**

I'm puzzled, the debug information didn't appear as expected! Upon re-examining the `test-sync-meta-ads.ts` output, a failure is evident: the cron sync returned a 401 status despite an HTTP 200 and reported an Unauthorized error. This is a bit unexpected.


**Pinpointing the Authentication Issue**

I've made a breakthrough. The Unauthorized error stems from the `if (!userId)` block, meaning `isServiceRole` was false and `userId` was undefined. This realization sparked a new question: why was `isServiceRole` false? My current hypothesis is a discrepancy between the `.env` value of `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` locally and the environment Deno is using. I am investigating.


**Investigating Service Role Keys**

I am now delving into the intricacies of the `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY`. I've decoded the JWT to confirm its validity for the correct project. However, the puzzle persists: why the discrepancy in the remote environment? If the key was different, how did the local admin client successfully create a temporary user and modify `meta_config` using this very key? The success of these operations, as noted in the test output, seems to contradict my hypothesis. I will explore this apparent contradiction further.

<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Identifying Encryption Targets**

I've pinpointed the exact columns for encryption now. The target columns are `long_lived_token` and `pages` within `meta_config`, along with `access_token` and `refresh_token` from `social_accounts`. Finally, I'm including `groq_api_key` in `user_api_keys`.


**Expanding Encryption Scope**

I've expanded the encryption targets to include a wider range of API keys: `openrouter_api_key`, `openai_api_key`, `elevenlabs_api_key`, `google_api_key`, `nanobana_api_key`, `seedance_api_key`, `wp_app_password`, `replicate_api_key`, `higgsfield_api_key`, and `higgsfield_api_key_secret`. I'm now wrestling with the migration plan and how to implement a transparent encryption/decryption process, specifically focusing on the implications of changing column names or types within the existing tables and the impact on existing queries. Backwards compatibility is a key concern.


**Considering Encryption Workflows**

I'm now focusing on how to handle encryption/decryption workflows. Specifically, I'm questioning whether we can store decrypted values only in the backend. I'm also considering changing column types to `UUID` referencing `vault.secrets(id)`. I'm concerned about how client-side operations would work, as PostgreSQL type-checking occurs before triggers, meaning the client would have to provide a valid UUID. I'm exploring if there's a way to allow plaintext key input from clients while automatically encrypting them within the database.


**Refining Encryption Implementation**

I'm now refining the implementation plan. I've decided to rename the original columns (e.g., `long_lived_token` to `long_lived_token_secret_id UUID REFERENCES vault.secrets(id)`) and create a view with the original name. The plan involves using a trigger to encrypt values using `pgsodium` or standard symmetric encryption in Postgres. I'm exploring the `vault.create_secret()` function to generate and store secret IDs. Finally, I'm thinking about storing the vault secret UUID (as a string) in the `TEXT` column (e.g. `

TRIGGERS: Intercept writes and store credentials in vault.secrets\\n-- \u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\\n\\n-- 1.1 meta_config encryption trigger\\nCREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION public.trg_encrypt_meta_config()\\nRETURNS TRIGGER AS $$\\nDECLARE\\n  v_token_id UUID;\\n  v_pages_id UUID;\\nBEGIN\\n  IF NEW.long_lived_token IS NOT NULL AND (TG_OP = 'INSERT' OR NEW.long_lived_token IS DISTINCT FROM OLD.long_lived_token) THEN\\n    IF NEW.long_lived_token ~ '^[0-9a-f]{8}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{12}$' THEN\\n      -- keep existing UUID\\n    ELSE\\n      INSERT INTO vault.secrets (secret, name, description)\\n      VALUES (NEW.long_lived_token, 'meta_config_long_lived_token', 'Meta config long-lived token')\\n      RETURNING id INTO v_token_id;\\n      NEW.long_lived_token = v_token_id::text;\\n    END IF;\\n  END IF;\\n\\n  IF NEW.pages IS NOT NULL AND (TG_OP = 'INSERT' OR NEW.pages IS DISTINCT FROM OLD.pages) THEN\\n    IF NEW.pages::text ~ '^\\\"[0-9a-f]{8}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{12}\\\"$' THEN\\n      -- keep existing UUID JSON\\n    ELSE\\n      INSERT INTO vault.secrets (secret, name, description)\\n      VALUES (NEW.pages::text, 'meta_config_pages', 'Meta config pages list')\\n      RETURNING id INTO v_pages_id;\\n      NEW.pages = to_jsonb(v_pages_id::text);\\n    END IF;\\n  END IF;\\n\\n  RETURN NEW;\\nEND;\\n$$ LANGUAGE plpgsql SECURITY DEFINER;\\n\\nCREATE TRIGGER tr_encrypt_meta_config\\n  BEFORE INSER\n<truncated 22281 bytes>",

**Investigating a Permission Error**

I've hit a snag. A permission denied error is popping up specifically for the `_crypto_aead_det_noncegen` function. I'm trying to figure out the root cause. My initial suspicion is that the database role executing the migration, like `postgres` or `supabase_admin`, lacks the necessary privileges.


**Analyzing Cryptography Permissions**

I'm now zeroing in on the `social_accounts` backfill's failure. It appears the `meta_config` backfill succeeded because it was empty, thus avoiding the `INSERT` trigger into `vault.secrets` and subsequent `pgsodium` function call. The `social_accounts` backfill, however, has data, triggering the failing `INSERT`. I'm also puzzled by the permission denied for the `postgres` user on the `_crypto_aead_det_noncegen` function. This requires further investigation.

</details>

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Discovering a Constraint**

I just encountered a constraint! Supabase Vault enforces uniqueness on secret names (secrets_name_idx), so I now understand why I got that error. I'm adapting to the enforced secret naming rule, to accommodate the new security architecture. I'm exploring the implications on my naming strategy, considering how I will structure the access tokens.


**Refining Unique Secret Names**

I've got it! I realized the secret name needs to be unique. I figured out how to make each secret name distinct by appending either the row ID or user ID, depending on the table. For example, `'social_accounts_access_token_' || r.id::text`. For `meta_config`, I'm appending user or config ID, like `'meta_config_long_lived_token_' || r.id::text`. I've confirmed that the ID is populated in the trigger.

</details>

TRIGGERS: Intercept writes and store credentials in vault.secrets\\n-- \u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\\n\\n-- 1.1 meta_config encryption trigger\\nCREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION public.trg_encrypt_meta_config()\\nRETURNS TRIGGER AS $$\\nDECLARE\\n  v_token_id UUID;\\n  v_pages_id UUID;\\nBEGIN\\n  IF NEW.id IS NULL THEN\\n    NEW.id := gen_random_uuid();\\n  END IF;\\n\\n  IF NEW.long_lived_token IS NOT NULL AND (TG_OP = 'INSERT' OR NEW.long_lived_token IS DISTINCT FROM OLD.long_lived_token) THEN\\n    IF NEW.long_lived_token ~ '^[0-9a-f]{8}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{12}$' THEN\\n      -- keep existing UUID\\n    ELSE\\n      v_token_id := vault.create_secret(NEW.long_lived_token, 'meta_config_long_lived_token_' || NEW.id::text, 'Meta config long-lived token');\\n      NEW.long_lived_token = v_token_id::text;\\n    END IF;\\n  END IF;\\n\\n  IF NEW.pages IS NOT NULL AND (TG_OP = 'INSERT' OR NEW.pages IS DISTINCT FROM OLD.pages) THEN\\n    IF NEW.pages::text ~ '^\\\"[0-9a-f]{8}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{12}\\\"$' THEN\\n      -- keep existing UUID JSON\\n    ELSE\\n      v_pages_id := vault.create_secret(NEW.pages::text, 'meta_config_pages_' || NEW.id::text, 'Meta config pages list');\\n      NEW.pages = to_jsonb(v_pages_id::text);\\n    END IF;\\n  END IF;\\n\\n  RETURN NEW;\\nEND;\\n$$ LANGUAGE plpgsql SECURITY DEFINER;\\n\\nCREATE OR REPLACE TRIGGER tr_encrypt_meta_config\\n  BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE ON public.me\n<truncated 21214 bytes>",

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Discovering User API Key Queries**

I've made a discovery: numerous parts of the system are querying `user_api_keys`. This presents an interesting challenge. With so many dependencies (approximately 18 files!), I'm now exploring methods to make access completely transparent, which could eliminate the need for extensive edits across the codebase.


**Refining Solution Architecture**

I'm zeroing in on a zero-intrusion approach. Renaming the base table to `user_api_keys_table` and the view to `user_api_keys` appears promising. My latest idea is to create an `INSTEAD OF` trigger on the view, so updates, inserts and deletes on it can automatically route to the underlying table. This would mean no edits to the 18 Edge Functions! This could be the perfect solution! I'm now testing the viability of this strategy.

</details>

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Analyzing API Keys**

I've been examining the `SettingsPage.tsx` file, specifically lines 158-181. I'm focusing on how API keys are handled. I see a `useEffect` hook that seems to be responsible for setting the API keys. It appears to check for `userKeys` and then sets the Groq, OpenRouter, and OpenAI API keys accordingly. I'll need to explore how `userKeys` is populated.


**Investigating Key Masking**

I've realized a critical UX flaw: the `SettingsPage` displays UUIDs (secret identifiers) directly in the API key input fields, since that's what `useUserApiKeys` pulls from the database. This would be a terrible user experience! While the database trigger correctly handles the UUIDs, the frontend exposure is unacceptable. I'm now exploring how to mask these fields using RLS policies, so that the user receives `NULL` or some other placeholder instead of the UUIDs when querying their keys, to improve usability and security.


**Implementing Key Redaction**

I've hit a conceptual breakthrough. RLS can't directly rewrite selected columns, as I first hoped. However, I now believe I have a superior solution: I can revoke `SELECT` access on key columns from `anon` and `authenticated` roles. This way, the frontend will receive `NULL` for these columns when querying data, which is ideal! The UI will display blank fields, emulating standard write-only API key inputs, just like a WordPress App Password. This handles both UX and security concerns beautifully.


**Addressing Key Overwrites**

I've uncovered a severe bug. My initial key redaction implementation, which replaces the UUIDs with `NULL` on the frontend, inadvertently leads to key overwrites. The `saveKeys` function sends empty strings ("") if the fields are `NULL`. The database trigger, incorrectly, interprets the empty strings as a signal to clear the keys. This would wipe out all saved keys! The current plaintext key storage masks this flaw. I need to fix this immediately.


**Refining Key Security**

I'm now devising a robust solution to handle key updates wit
<truncated 1453 bytes>
Furthermore, the `WITH (security_invoker = true)` setting enforces RLS on the base table. The user-facing view operates without joining the `vault.decrypted_secrets` table, as it's not needed, improving security! This design is robust and easy to comprehend!


**Extending Masking Strategy**

I'm now extending the robust masking approach across other sensitive data. I'm focusing on `meta_config` and `social_accounts`, implementing the same secure view and trigger strategy as the API keys. This ensures consistent data protection throughout the database. I'm renaming the tables and creating views with a `'••••••••••••'` mask to protect long-lived tokens, access tokens, and refresh tokens. This is a big step! I have confirmed zero errors, and it is elegant!


**Confirming Referential Integrity**

I'm now 100% confident in the safety of renaming the sensitive data tables. I've verified that no other tables reference `meta_config`, `social_accounts`, or `user_api_keys`, so renaming them won't cause any referential integrity issues. The RLS policies, triggers, and even `security_invoker = true` will all be preserved. I can start working on a migration file now!


**Refining Deletion Logic**

I'm now refining the deletion handling for `meta_config` within the `INSTEAD OF` trigger. I need to ensure deletions are correctly propagated to the underlying table, while safeguarding sensitive data. I'm modifying the trigger to include a `DELETE` condition, so now, when the view receives a deletion request, it correctly removes the corresponding record from the base table. I'm testing this new logic. This is an important consideration!



**Validating Trigger Mechanics**

I'm now carefully examining the triggers, confirming they elegantly manage `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, and `DELETE` operations. I've validated the logic, focusing on the `COALESCE` and `NULLIF` patterns to maintain key security. Specifically, the triggers for `social_accounts` and `user_api_keys` are now robust! The `DELETE` logic is verified!

**Verifying Deployment Details**

I've pinpointed the project's remote database: `https://bcyvddsykvehvpwstlfa.supabase.co`. My next step is to examine `whatsapp-webhook` within this remote project, specifically checking its deployment status. I'm now verifying if the deployment loop successfully handled it, along with the other 21 functions.


**Analyzing Security Implications**

I've discovered that while `whatsapp-webhook` wasn't directly modified, its reliance on `meta_config` introduces a critical vulnerability. The renaming of base tables combined with the `security_invoker = true` attribute on the `meta_config` view indicates a potential RLS bypass due to the `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` used in `admin`. I must now thoroughly investigate the RLS policies on the underlying `meta_config_table` to assess the actual security risk, especially since the function uses the database's `service_role` authorization header.


**Investigating Permission Bypass**

I'm now diving deep into the implications of `security_invoker = true` on the `meta_config` view. It seems the edge function's use of `service_role` and `BYPASSRLS` is the crux of the matter. While the function can bypass RLS on *tables*, I need to clarify whether this bypass extends to views with `security_invoker = true`. The test script's output gave no errors on insert, so I'm investigating the test script to see if the insert was actually successful.

</details>

  "CodeContent": "\"-- Fix Instead-of trigger on meta_config view to handle NULLs for NOT NULL columns with defaults\\nCREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION public.trg_instead_of_meta_config()\\nRETURNS TRIGGER AS $$\\nBEGIN\\n  IF TG_OP = 'DELETE' THEN\\n    DELETE FROM public.meta_config_table WHERE id = OLD.id;\\n    RETURN OLD;\\n  ELSIF TG_OP = 'INSERT' THEN\\n    INSERT INTO public.meta_config_table (\\n      id,\\n      user_id,\\n      facebook_user_id,\\n      long_lived_token,\\n      scopes,\\n      pages,\\n      instagram_business_account_id,\\n      whatsapp_phone_number_id,\\n      whatsapp_waba_id,\\n      meta_ad_account_id,\\n      requires_reauth,\\n      token_expires_at\\n    ) VALUES (\\n      COALESCE(NEW.id, gen_random_uuid()),\\n      NEW.user_id,\\n <details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

**Investigating Database Permissions**

I've hit a snag. The authenticated user can't select data from the `meta_config` view, getting a "permission denied" error for `meta_config_table`. The view definition, shown in the SQL, points at a table and I'm guessing there's a problem there. Now, the next step is to examine the user's roles and permissions for that specific table.


**Analyzing Permission Revocation**

I've got it! The `REVOKE` and `GRANT` statements were targeting the original `meta_config` table, and the subsequent rename to `meta_config_table` did not transfer these permissions to the view `meta_config`. I was looking at the SQL scripts and realizing the column-level grants were on the original table, then renamed. The view was created after the rename and doesn't explicitly inherit the grants. I've confirmed that the view `meta_config` was created without granting select permissions to `anon` and `authenticated`.


**Confirming View Permissions**

I'm now zeroing in on the crux of the matter. My user lacks SELECT privileges on the underlying table `meta_config_table`, and I am now certain the query fails due to this. I've re-examined the error message and the view definition to corroborate my assumption. To be certain, I am reexamining the existing configuration with regards to view creation and default privileges to see if the default behavior is impacting the outcome. And it appears that the view uses `security_invoker = true`, which means the authenticated user needs SELECT privileges on both the view and the underlying table. Now I'm checking which columns of `meta_config_table` are selected by the view.


**Unveiling Column-Level Impacts**

I've made a breakthrough, or rather, realized an extremely elegant PostgreSQL design aspect! The view `meta_config` selects specific columns, including `long_lived_token` and `pages`, from `meta_config_table`. The `authenticated` role lacks SELECT privilege on these columns on `meta_config_table`, so the query fails. It's a protection mechanism to protect t
<truncated 393 bytes>
mprovement! I realized the `security_invoker = true` setting on the view is unnecessary if I apply a `WHERE` clause in the view definition that filters by the user ID. This approach provides row-level security without granting direct access to the underlying table. The view, when `security_invoker` is not specified, runs as the definer (e.g. `postgres`), which bypasses the requirement for the authenticated user to have select privileges on `meta_config_table`. By explicitly filtering on `user_id = auth.uid()`, the view secures the data and handles access control!


**Optimizing View Definitions**

I've just had a major realization, I can eliminate `security_invoker = true` from my views and apply a `WHERE user_id = auth.uid()` clause! Doing this means that the client needs no access to the underlying table. Then, I can grant access solely on the views, providing row-level security. The elegance of this design is that the view will run as the definer, which is the system user, and the system user is allowed to select all data. This also applies to `social_accounts` and `user_api_keys`. It seems that all clients will only query for data belonging to `auth.uid()`, since the API Tenancy Model dictates that. Now, let's explore how Deno Edge Functions and service role queries impact the proposed solution, and what those functions are querying.


**Refining Security Implementation**

I've made a crucial discovery. My recent work to refine the view definitions by introducing `WHERE` clauses will need a significant adjustment for service role access. I've realized that if I apply the `WHERE user_id = auth.uid()` clause to filter the results, the admin role won't work! Therefore, I must use `WHERE (auth.uid() = user_id OR auth.role() = 'service_role')` to allow the service role to bypass the user ID filter and update any rows. I've confirmed that `auth.role() = 'service_role'` works as expected with Supabase. Finally, I will ensure proper access with `GRANT SELECT ON ...` for views and `REVOKE SELECT ON ...` for tables.

</details>
